On this page

  • § 1. Why now — what was hunted, and what was rejected
  • § 2. The engine's own work
  • § 3. Hand pass, by case file
  • Case file: State Farm Arena — "suitcases of ballots pulled from under a table" (Georgia, Nov 3, 2020)
  • 1. The containers held already-opened, not-yet-counted absentee ballots, sealed and stored under the table about 10 p.m. in front of monitors and media
  • 2. The timeline: box under the table at 10:29 p.m. with the media present; media and observers leave at 10:46; boxes come out and scanning resumes at 11:03
  • 3. The table came in empty that morning, and no ballots were brought in from outside
  • 4. FBI, GBI and Secretary of State investigators reviewed the entire unedited footage and found no fraud
  • 5. The State Election Board dismissed the "poll worker fraud" case on June 20, 2023
  • 6. The containers were ballot carriers sealed with tamper-proof seals, not suitcases
  • 7. The "water leak" was an overflowing urinal that morning and did not affect the evening count
  • 8. The social-media "confession" attributed to one of the workers was fake
  • 9. A court later found the tapes show no illegal activity, and a state official had called it "normal ballot processing" on Dec 4, 2020
  • 10. The former President's personal lawyer, who spread the clip, was disbarred in New York on July 2, 2024
  • 11. The two election workers shown in the clip won "about $148 million" in defamation damages
  • Case file: the observers and the state monitor at State Farm Arena
  • 12. "Both Republican watchers' affidavits confirmed no one was told to leave"
  • 13. The State Election Board's monitor was out of the room from about 8:15 p.m. to 11:52 p.m.
  • 14. The 9,752 extra ballots were scanned while the monitor was out (8:15 to 11:52 p.m.)
  • 15. The decision to keep counting came from above, around 10:30 p.m.
  • 16. No rule required counting to pause when the watchers left
  • Case file: the rescan clip — "the same stack of ballots scanned over and over" (Fulton, 2020)
  • 17. Running a batch through again after a mis-scan is standard procedure
  • 18. In Georgia's voting system, rejecting a batch deletes its tallied votes but keeps its images, so duplicate images can exist without duplicate votes
  • 19. The rescan clip is pure misreading: nothing was double-counted
  • 20. A fact-checker's "0.153%" hand-count gap is a typo for the state's 0.1053%
  • Case file: what the paper said — Georgia's 2020 hand count
  • 21. The hand count covered about 5.0 million ballots in 41,881 batches across all 159 counties
  • 22. The gap was 0.1053% of votes and 0.0099% of the margin, against an expected 1.0–1.5% from human counting error
  • 23. A risk-limiting audit confirms the winner, not the exact margin
  • 24. Fulton: the hand count found 525,293 ballots (634 more than first reported), a 243,559-vote margin, and a net shift of 345 votes toward the losing candidate
  • 25. No county's margin moved more than 0.73%, and 103 of 159 moved less than 0.05%
  • 26. Forgotten memory cards in four counties (Fayette, Floyd, Walton, Douglas) netted about 1,200 votes toward the losing candidate
  • 27. Georgia recertified on Dec 7, 2020 after the machine recount
  • 28. The recount margin was 11,779, and Fulton's recount (523,779) was the least complete of its three counts
  • Case file: Fulton 2020 was badly run — not shown to be rigged
  • 29. The court-ordered monitor saw no fraud but "disorganization, sloppiness, and mismanagement"
  • 30. The state's Performance Review Board (Jan 13, 2023) found no evidence of fraud or of anything that would have changed the result, and pointed to three matching counts
  • 31. In May 2024 the State Election Board found 3,075 duplicate ballot images in the recount, reprimanded the county 2-1 and required a 2024 monitor
  • 32. On Aug 6, 2024 the board voted 3-2 to refer the case to the Attorney General
  • 33. The Attorney General's office declined on Aug 19, 2024, saying the board cannot direct it to investigate
  • 34. The 2024 monitors, working with the Carter Center, found Fulton's 2024 election "organized and orderly"
  • Case file: the 2026 FBI seizure, the affidavit, and the judge's order (the peg)
  • 35. The FBI seized Fulton's 2020 ballots on Jan 29, 2026
  • 36. Agents took 656 boxes
  • 37. The affidavit says some allegations were disproven and some substantiated, and names "unknown persons"
  • 38. The affidavit lists five real defects: missing images for 528,777 and 527,925 ballots, rescans, inconsistent hand-audit batch tallies, uncreased absentee ballots, and a 511,343 recount figure
  • 39. The "40%" figure: the duplicated ballots leaned toward the losing candidate
  • 40. The judge called the affidavit "defective in some respects", the 17,434 figure "misleading", and the whole "far from perfect", but said no officer "intentionally lied"
  • 41. The order is a narrow ruling on returning property, not a finding about fraud
  • 42. The referral came from the presidentially appointed Director of Election Security and Integrity, on Jan 5, 2026
  • 43. Georgia did not require counties to keep ballot images in 2020
  • 44. The state had already examined the 17,434 "shortfall": the complainants conflated ballots with votes, and the late report came from a scanner programming error
  • 45. The return-of-property case is closed, with no appeal on the docket
  • 46. No one has been charged since the seizure
  • 47. The statute of limitations is five years, and the judge pointed to an image-modification date within the period
  • Case file: the State Election Board's letters, and Georgia's voting-machine and QR-code record
  • 48. The letters are dated Sept 30, 2026, from the board's executive director to the Attorney General and the Homeland Security Secretary
  • 49. A Sept 28 board resolution authorised seeking federal help (reported as a 3-2 vote), and the board's chair says the letters went beyond it
  • 50. Georgia's QR-code tabulation ban takes effect July 1, 2026 (as the letters say)
  • 51. The county election officials' association letter asking the board to rescind is dated Oct 8, 2026
  • 52. Advance voting in Georgia starts Oct 13, 2026
  • 53. The Justice Department said only that it was "aware of the letter"
  • 54. CISA's June 2022 advisory listed nine vulnerabilities in the touchscreen model Georgia uses, and found no evidence any was exploited in an election
  • 55. The federal suit over Georgia's touchscreen machines was dismissed for lack of standing on Mar 31, 2025, after a 17-day trial, with the judge calling the security concerns "substantial"
  • 56. "The case has since been dismissed" and is over
  • 57. SB 189 (2024) makes the printed text, not the QR code, the official vote for tabulation
  • 58. SB 3EX (2026) also creates a committee to specify a new system based on hand-marked paper ballots, reporting by Jan 31, 2027
  • 59. Georgia's 2024 ballot-image audit found zero differences between the QR codes and the printed text
  • 60. The board's letters lean on a Sept 2, 2026 Homeland Security letter asking the Justice Department whether QR-code tabulation complies with federal law
  • Case file: Wisconsin — the "ballots under the table" memory, and Wisconsin's real items
  • 61. There was no separate Wisconsin "ballots under a table" clip; that story is the Georgia footage
  • 62. Wisconsin's 2020 viral claim was "found 112k votes at 3 a.m." / "a 4 a.m. ballot dump"
  • 63. In the Nov 2020 Milwaukee recount, 386 uncounted absentee ballots were found underneath counted envelopes
  • 64. Nov 2024: a clip of trucks arriving overnight at Milwaukee's central count showed illegal votes
  • 65. Nov 2022: a Madison poll worker initialling ballots was relabelled as "cheating in Philadelphia"
  • 66. Wisconsin's Legislative Audit Bureau (2021): 3 of 14,710 sampled absentee certificates lacked a voter signature
  • 67. Eleven people who died before Nov 3, 2020 likely voted; their absentee ballots had already been mailed
  • 68. The state commission's care-facility guidance did not comply with statutes
  • 69. "1 of 45 sworn complaints sustained"
  • 70. The commission's rebuttal on care-facility ballots: 579 issued and 389 returned in 2020, against 810 and 633 in 2016
  • 71. The legislature's special counsel conceded that decertification "would not" change the result
  • Case file: Detroit (TCF Center, 2020) and Philadelphia — "observers locked out, windows covered, a 4 a.m. ballot dump"
  • 72. The overnight delivery was about 16,000 Election Day absentee ballots
  • 73. The van arrived about 3:30 a.m. and again about 4:30 a.m.; the "wagon" photo was a TV photographer's gear
  • 74. The Republican-led Senate committee found no fraud, but called window-covering and removals by both sides "not always lawful or wise"
  • 75. The committee recommended chain-of-custody manifests for ballots moved to the count
  • 76. More than 570 challengers were in the room (227 Republican) against a cap of 134 per party, and late arrivals of both parties were held at the door
  • 77. The state audit reconciled Detroit's absentee count to within 21 of about 174,000
  • 78. The audit found challenges were documented, not ignored
  • 79. A Wayne County court (Nov 13, 2020) found the challengers' affidavits misread the process; no challenger affiant had attended the Oct 29 walk-through
  • 80. The related federal suit ended in sanctions on its lawyers, reduced on appeal
  • 81. Philadelphia 2020: Pennsylvania's Supreme Court held (Nov 17, 2020) that the election code sets no minimum distance for observers
  • Case file: the New York-to-Pennsylvania "truck of completed ballots" (Oct 2020)
  • 82. The viral figure was 250,000–300,000 ballots; the driver's own sworn affidavit said 250 to 7,500
  • 83. A joint OIG, Postal Inspection Service and FBI investigation, with 20+ interviews, could not corroborate it; closed Feb 8, 2022
  • 84. The trailer held parcels, its seal matched, and every trailer is accounted for
  • 85. The comparator: the origin facility's election-mail log held about 150 ballot images for the whole season
  • 86. The only large New York-to-Pennsylvania ballot flow found was printed general-election ballots from a Rochester printer
  • Case file: "2000 Mules" (2022) — drop-box video and phone tracking
  • 87. The state's bureau of investigation found the phone data "curious" but short of probable cause
  • 88. The State Election Board dismissed three drop-box cases (May 17, 2022): household members returning family ballots
  • 89. The voter shown in the film returned ballots for himself and four family members
  • 90. The publisher apologised (May 31, 2024) and pulled the film and book
  • 91. The film-maker's side conceded the drop-box videos were never linked to geolocation data
  • 92. The court (Sept 30, 2025) recorded that the defendants did not contest that the "ballot mule" accusations were false; the voter was never geotracked
  • 93. The film-maker settled; "July 30" against "before an August 2026 trial"
  • 94. The group behind the analysis told a court it had no names or documents, and the State Election Board dropped its enforcement suit (Feb 26, 2025)
  • 95. Arizona's Attorney General's investigators also never received the group's data
  • 96. The film made over $9 million
  • Case file: Bucks County, Pennsylvania — the "ripped-up mail ballots" video (Oct 2024)
  • 97. US intelligence, the FBI and CISA said (Oct 25, 2024) that Russian actors made and amplified it, and local officials had already debunked it
  • 98. The county board's bipartisan statement the same day: the video is fake
  • 99. "Later" the district attorney's office and Yardley Borough police said their investigation found it fabricated
  • 100. The physical tells: glossy instead of matte, the wrong green, no return addresses, no punch hole; and Pennsylvania bars opening mail ballots before 7 a.m. on Election Day
  • 101. It was debunked within three hours
  • 102. It was staged, not made with AI
  • 103. It reached hundreds of thousands of views on X
  • 104. The "Storm-1516" label comes from an outside researcher, by account history
  • Case file: the Georgia "Haitian voters" video (Oct 31, 2024) and the Arizona "overseas ballots" video (Nov 4, 2024)
  • 105. US intelligence, the FBI and CISA said (Nov 1, 2024) that Russian influence actors made the Georgia video
  • 106. No voter registration exists for the names shown; four licences carry the same photo and lack the state's red marks
  • 107. The Secretary of State's office called it false and probably foreign (Oct 31, 2024)
  • 108. The account that first posted it told a network it had been paid by an unknown source
  • 109. It was staged, not made with AI
  • 110. The video drew over half a million views
  • 111. The Arizona "overseas ballots" video showed fake ballots
  • Case file: "machines flipped votes" — Antrim County 2020, the machine maker's lawsuit, and the 2024 audits
  • 112. Antrim County's hand count came "within 12 votes of 15,718"
  • 113. The scanners' own paper tapes were right on election night
  • 114. The cause was human error in loading memory cards, not a security breach, and the county restated its results four times
  • 115. The Senate committee called the Antrim manipulation claims "indefensible" and gave their promoters "zero credibility"
  • 116. Wisconsin 2024: 327,230 ballots hand-counted, a 0.0% machine error rate, decided unanimously
  • 117. Georgia's 2024 ballot-image audit: zero QR-versus-text differences, and "86 intent differences plus 1 write-in error" on the hand-marked ballots
  • 118. Georgia's 2024 risk-limiting audit: 442 batches, 381 with no deviation at all
  • 119. Pennsylvania's audits found about six discrepancies in about 37,000 hand-counted ballots
  • 120. The machine maker's suit against a cable network: summary judgment on falsity (Mar 31, 2023), then a $787.5 million settlement (Apr 18, 2023)
  • Case file: Arizona 2020 — "Sharpiegate" and the state Senate's contractor review
  • 121. Sharpies were officially issued, and the Attorney General's office said it was "now confident" they disenfranchised no one
  • 122. Maricopa County rated the contractor's 76 claims: 22 misleading, 41 inaccurate, 13 false
  • 123. Of 53,304 ballots the contractor questioned, 87 were potentially illegal, and 37 possible double votes went to the Attorney General
  • 124. The contractor's own hand count "largely matched", the Senate's machine count "confirms" the county's total, and the parties' hand-count audit had found a 100% match
  • 125. The Attorney General's special investigations memo (Sept 19, 2022): 638 complaints, 430 investigations, 22 cases; one accidental deceased-voter instance and two double-voting cases sent for prosecution
  • 126. The next Attorney General released that memo on Feb 22, 2023
  • 127. The Attorney General's earlier public interim letter (Apr 6, 2022) read differently: "serious vulnerabilities", and 100,000–200,000 ballots moved without proper chain of custody
  • 128. The losing campaign's Maricopa suit hinged on 191 presidential overvotes and was withdrawn as moot
  • Case file: the US Postal Service in 2020 — "sorting machines and mailboxes pulled to stop mail ballots" (the claim from the other side)
  • 129. 712 sorting machines were removed from Oct 2019 to Aug 2020, against about 375 a year, 437 of them in June–August
  • 130. The changes were made without a study of the impact on service, to hit financial targets
  • 131. 776 of over 140,000 blue collection boxes were removed in summer 2020; the Western area jumped from 8 a month to 187 in August; removals were suspended Aug 18
  • 132. On-time mail fell about 10 points in July 2020
  • 133. Election mail then ran better than other mail: 93.8% on time against 88.1%, 98.1% in election week, and the 760 delayed pieces found were all delivered by Election Day
  • 134. USPS's own figure: 99.89% of ballots delivered within seven days
  • Case file: 2024 from the other side — "20 million missing votes", Starlink, "statistical anomalies"
  • 135. Certified totals: the losing 2024 ticket got 75,017,613 votes of 155,238,302; the winning 2020 ticket had 81,268,924 of 158,383,403, a gap of about 6.25 million
  • 136. The "20 million" came from partial counts, and the same number was used both ways
  • 137. State boards said satellite internet was not used to tabulate or upload counts, and CISA saw no material malicious activity
  • 138. Satellite internet connected pollbooks only in Tulare County, California
  • 139. The "statistical anomalies" group has no confirming audit, recount or ruling
  • 140. A 10.54% vs 1.07% down-ballot drop-off in Clark County, Nevada
  • Case file: dead voters (Georgia 2020)
  • 141. Jan 6, 2021: 10,315 dead voters alleged; the state had found 2 potential cases
  • 142. Dec 14, 2021: two confirmed of 40 suspected in one complaint, and a board member's running tally of "five"
  • 143. The final count of dead-voter ballots is unsettled: 2, 4 or 5
  • 144. The lawyer who spread the claim later stipulated it was false; the 10,315 came from matching on birth year only
  • 145. The "5,000" figure came from the then-President's own recorded call of Jan 2, 2021
  • 146. A federal letter now says citizenship checks found "more than 42,000 deceased registrants" on Georgia's 2026 rolls
  • Case file: stale voter rolls — real, lawful by design, and a roll entry is not a ballot
  • 147. In 2024 the rolls held about 234.5 million registrations against 158.2 million ballots counted
  • 148. 23.2 million (9.9%) registrations were inactive
  • 149. 21.3 million records were removed in 2022–24, about one in five because the voter had died
  • 150. 38.8 million confirmation notices were sent, 68.9% unreturned
  • 151. Georgia removed 466,063 records (5.7%), 146,022 for death, against 5,297,500 ballots counted
  • 152. The count already rejects mail ballots from voters who died
  • 153. Federal law: a mover can't be dropped without written confirmation, or a missed notice plus two federal general elections; removal for not voting is barred; systematic removals end 90 days before a federal election
  • 154. ERIC matches roll, licence, death and change-of-address data, reports to its member states, and never touches the rolls; 27 states and DC are members
  • 155. Pew (2012): about 24 million inaccurate registrations, 1.8 million deceased people listed, 2.75 million people registered in more than one state
  • Case file: noncitizens on the rolls — registered is not voted
  • 156. Georgia's October 2024 citizenship audit found 20 noncitizens registered out of 8.2 million
  • 157. Georgia, Sept 1, 2026: "432 potential noncitizens who have voted at some point since 1996"
  • 158. Utah's citizenship review (final report May 27, 2026): 27 confirmed noncitizens registered, 13 of whom had voted, out of 2,069,640 records
  • 159. The federal SAVE system, run against state rolls in 2025–26, verified 65 million voters and flagged 28,635 as potential noncitizens
  • 160. Texas ran 18 million registrations through SAVE: 2,724 flagged, 578 later shown to be citizens, 117 cases referred for investigation
  • 161. SAVE has flagged real citizens
  • Case file: the Justice Department's 2025–26 suits for states' full voter rolls
  • 162. 31 suits (30 states plus DC): 26 lost, Oklahoma resolved, 4 pending (Delaware, Hawaii, Idaho, Utah)
  • 163. The Sixth Circuit (Michigan) affirmed dismissal 2–1 on June 24, 2026
  • 164. Georgia's refiled suit was dismissed Sept 30, 2026: the judge held the 1960 Act does not authorise the demand or preempt state privacy law, and that Georgia's public roll for a fee "would be enough"
  • 165. Maryland: the judge "joins every court to have addressed this issue"
  • Case file: North Carolina's 9th District, 2018 — real, proven, organised fraud
  • 166. The bipartisan State Board voted 5-0 for a new election; it could "not determine who would have won", and the irregularities tainted the whole race
  • 167. The operation submitted at least 788 absentee request forms in Bladen County and at least 231 in Robeson, and paid workers $125 per 50 ballots collected
  • 168. The margin was 905 votes; mail-in absentee votes in the two counties' parts of the district totalled 1,364
  • 169. Unreturned absentee ballots: Bladen 595 of 1,323 (44.97%); Robeson 1,493 of 2,269 (65.80%)
  • 170. It was caught by investigators and records, not by a viral clip; the Board refused to certify on Nov 27, 2018
  • 171. The prosecutions ended in misdemeanour pleas without jail; the operative died before trial; the candidate was not charged
  • Case file: Bridgeport, Connecticut, 2023 — real ballot-stuffing on camera, proven in court
  • 172. The primary's margin was 251 votes (4,212 to 3,961); the challenger won the in-person vote and the incumbent the absentee vote
  • 173. The footage: 21 days of drop-box video, 420 approaches, several people dropping multiple ballots per trip
  • 174. Two campaign figures, the town party's vice-chair and a council candidate, took the Fifth; the court found they made multiple drops of multiple ballots
  • 175. The ruling (Nov 1, 2023) ordered a new primary, with the result "in serious doubt"
  • 176. Up to 1,253 drop-box ballots against about 420 people on 2,100 hours of video
  • 177. In the court-ordered rerun (Jan 23, 2024), the incumbent won again
  • 178. Nine people were charged over the 2023 cycle after a state elections-commission referral; two rejected plea deals in Aug 2026; no verdict yet
  • 179. Bridgeport 2023 as the strongest drop-box case (as held by hand pass C)
  • Case file: Paterson, New Jersey, May 2020 — a voided all-mail council election
  • 180. It began when postal inspectors found hundreds of ballots bundled in mailboxes
  • 181. About 19% of the city's mail ballots were rejected, and the ward winner led by about 240
  • 182. A Superior Court judge (Aug 19, 2020) set the result aside as "rife with mail in vote procedural violations", leaving fraud to the criminal courts
  • 183. The court-ordered rerun (Nov 2020) was won by the same challenger, by 14 votes
  • 184. Four men were charged in June 2020; as of October 2026 neither councilman has been convicted
  • 185. "The county board decided not to count 800 ballots"
  • Case file: the scale of proven fraud nationally — real, rare, mostly one person acting alone
  • 186. Heritage's database lists 1,561 "proven instances" (about 85% convictions) across decades, says it is a sample, and says "nobody really knows the extent"
  • 187. AP's Dec 2021 review of six disputed 2020 states found fewer than 475 potential cases against a combined 311,257-vote margin, nearly all one person acting alone
  • 188. Brookings: Arizona's Heritage-listed fraud rate is .0000845% of ballots over 25 years, and "no election outcome in the U.S. has ever been altered by ballot fraud"
  • 189. The best evidence for "fraud is under-detected": a national estimate of double voting (Goel et al., American Political Science Review, 2020)
  • 190. Wisconsin's audit bureau (2021): 4 people may have voted twice by absentee ballot in 2020
  • 191. A 2017 Brennan Center critique of Heritage's database (10 in-person impersonation cases, and so on)
  • Case file: the 2020 close-out record — what the people in charge said, and what the courts decided
  • 192. The Attorney General at the time, Dec 1, 2020: no fraud on a scale that could have changed the outcome
  • 193. The election infrastructure councils convened by CISA, Nov 12, 2020: "the most secure in American history"
  • 194. The Supreme Court, Dec 11, 2020 (Texas v. Pennsylvania): denied for lack of standing
  • 195. A counted tally of the post-election lawsuits: 64 cases with 187 counts in six states, one minor win ("Lost, Not Stolen", July 2022)
  • Case file: what lawful ballot handling looks like on camera
  • 196. Georgia's 2020 drop-box rule: video of every box, two-person sworn collection, a signed transfer form, video kept 30 days
  • 197. Georgia's 2020 absentee-processing rule: scanned ballots go in sealed, labelled containers, in batches of at most 100
  • 198. Pennsylvania's guidance: a locked box with a numbered tamper-evident seal, video kept a year, two collectors (one a board employee), and a chain-of-custody form that travels with the ballots
  • 199. A Pennsylvania appeals court (Aug 6, 2025, non-precedential) upheld release of county election-building footage with faces blurred
  • Case file: how a viewer can check a clip — the method, and the research on whether people can use it
  • 200. Fact-checkers ask four questions (is it original, who posted it, where, when) and reverse-search key frames, because no free tool searches whole clips; an upload date is not a film date (attributed to Bellingcat, 2017)
  • 201. The professional checklist is First Draft's five pillars: provenance, source, date, location, motivation
  • 202. InVID/WeVerify splits a video into keyframes and reverse-searches them
  • 203. Professional fact-checkers read laterally (Wineburg and McGrew, Teachers College Record, 2019)
  • 204. High-school students taught lateral reading in six lessons (Wineburg et al., Journal of Educational Psychology, 2022)
  • 205. College students in a four-hour online module (Breakstone et al., HKS Misinformation Review, 2021)
  • 206. A pop-up reminding adults to read laterally (Panizza et al., Scientific Reports, 2022, 5,387 people)
  • 207. Prebunking videos run as YouTube ads (Roozenbeek et al., Science Advances, 2022)
  • 208. Inoculation has a published critique and a counter-analysis
  • 209. Short "tips to spot false news" (Guess et al., PNAS, 2020)
  • 210. Do corrections of election-fraud claims work?
  • 211. The federal "Rumor vs. Reality" page is gone
  • 212. What election offices tell voters to do: go to their own state and county offices
  • § 4. Top-up hand passes (2026-10-13, after the host's pitch questions)
  • Case file: proven fraud since 2024 — the case sweep
  • 213. The Justice Department charged at least 57 people with election crimes in six national releases between Sept 4 and Oct 12, 2026, mostly for noncitizen voting
  • 214. The federal cases are individual, not one scheme
  • 215. Maricopa County, Sept 28, 2026: of 60 voters flagged by the federal citizenship check, 52 were citizens and 8 were not; no evidence of an attempt to sway an election; 147 more still under review
  • 216. Florida, Sept 24, 2026: 20 indicted — 16 noncitizens who voted and 4 people who voted while on felony probation
  • 217. Frisco City, Alabama: three indicted; 20 illegal absentee ballots per court documents; a judge ordered a rerun by consent decree
  • 218. Ft. Deposit, Alabama: the August 2025 town election was set aside and rerun in January 2026; two people charged
  • 219. Mesa County, Colorado, 2024: 16 ballots forged, 12 caught by the signature check, 3 counted out of more than 94,600; the postal carrier got five years
  • 220. Atlantic City, New Jersey: a paid "messenger" mail-ballot scheme in the Nov 2022 election; 24 months in federal prison (July 23, 2025)
  • 221. Millbourne, Pennsylvania: three borough council officials registered about three dozen non-residents and cast their mail ballots; the candidate still lost by 27
  • 222. Frio County, Texas: 15 indicted for paid vote harvesting, officials among them; not-guilty pleas; no verdict found
  • 223. Pennsylvania, Oct 24, 2025: six paid canvassers and their director charged over fake registration forms; the Attorney General said the motive was money
  • 224. Ohio: 597 noncitizen cases referred in 2024 (138 had voted); 1,084 referred to the Justice Department in Oct 2025 (167 appear to have voted in a federal election since 2018); AP found 9 indictments from 621 referrals over ten years
  • 225. Harris County, Texas: a court voided the 2022 180th District Court race (about 1,430 illegal votes against a 449-vote margin); the incumbent resigned and dropped his appeal in Sept 2025
  • 226. Hamtramck, Michigan: two sitting council members charged (Aug 2025) over pre-signed absentee ballots; officials had sought help for years
  • 227. Single-voter cases still end in prison terms (Douglas County, Colorado, 3 years; Clay County, Alabama)
  • 228. Heritage's database has no 2026 entries; it was last updated Dec 12, 2025; it lists 55 entries for 2024–25
  • 229. Bridgeport, Oct 6, 2026: one person charged over absentee ballots in the August 2026 primary
  • 230. A national review (CEIR, via NPR, July 2025): noncitizen voting happens "in minuscule numbers"; 18 states disclosed nothing
  • Case file: the peg, updated to 2026-10-13
  • 231. No one has been charged in the FBI's Fulton County investigation as of Oct 13, 2026
  • 232. A grand-jury subpoena for the details of everyone who worked the 2020 Fulton count was quashed on July 7, 2026, and the Justice Department has appealed
  • 233. The Justice Department now says 2020 image files "may have been altered in 2024"
  • 234. The board director's Sept 30 letters stand; the Justice Department was only "aware of the letter"; no board meeting is posted before Dec 16
  • 235. Homeland Security's "more than 42,000 deceased registrants" in Georgia has no published method, and a roll entry is not a vote
  • Case file: do fraud stories "disappear"? Coverage, retractions and walk-backs
  • 236. The Attorney General said federal election-crime charges totalled 90, 50 of them for noncitizen voting, and complained the media did not take them seriously (Sept 30, 2026)
  • 237. Were Maricopa's eight charged?
  • 238. About 70 articles accusing the two Fulton County election workers were taken down after a defamation settlement
  • 239. Retractions of 2020 machine-fraud claims came through legal settlements, and were quieter than the claims
  • 240. A 2020 federal prosecutor's release on discarded military ballots in Luzerne County, Pennsylvania overstated the ballots and was corrected the same day; the decision not to charge was kept from the public until after the election
  • 241. The Texas Attorney General's page dropped its "155 individuals" prosecution tally between Apr 20 and Apr 30, 2023
  • 242. Big "potential noncitizen" numbers shrank on checking (Texas 2019, Alabama 2024)
  • 243. Florida's 2022 voter-fraud arrests were mixed, not a clean collapse
  • 244. YouTube removed claims of widespread 2020 fraud, then stopped (June 2023)
  • 245. A true, proven fraud story was taken down
  • 246. Newspaper attention to voter fraud does not track the number of cases
  • 247. Fact-checks are a sliver of the conversation and arrive days late (2022 midterms, on X)
  • 248. The Pennsylvania registration-form case was not buried: national coverage, charges, a guilty plea
  • 249. The coverage table: which outlets carried which of twelve cases
  • Case file: the states that "refuse to give the government their voter rolls"
  • 250. What the Justice Department demanded: the full list, with date of birth plus a driver's licence number or the last four digits of the Social Security number
  • 251. The statutes cited and the stated purpose: checking the state's list maintenance under federal voter-registration law
  • 252. The purpose given privately, and later in court, was wider: run the rolls against the federal SAVE citizenship check, looking for noncitizens and dead voters
  • 253. The data-sharing agreement states were asked to sign binds them to "clean" their rolls within 45 days of the department's notice, and lets contractors see the data
  • 254. "A national voter database"
  • 255. How many states gave the full file: about 16 to 18
  • 256. The state-by-state record: about 16 states gave the full file; 3 gave only the public file and were not sued; 30 states and DC were sued, 26 dismissed, Oklahoma settled, 4 pending
  • 257. The reasons the refusing states gave
  • 258. Does refusal split along party lines?
  • 259. In most states the voter file is public or can be bought; what is withheld is the sensitive identifiers
  • 260. The department could already get the public file, and many states sent it; Homeland Security bought and analysed public files itself
  • 261. Homeland Security's national SAVE totals (as of June 22, 2026): 68 million records from 25 states, "over 400,000 deceased registrants and over 28,000 non-citizens"
  • 262. The "more than 42,000 deceased registrants" figure came in a Homeland Security letter to Georgia
  • 263. Texas first announced its SAVE results on Sept 15, 2026
  • 264. Homeland Security: 250,000 noncitizens registered in four states (July 2026)
  • 265. A real failure was found by a state that refused the federal demand: New Jersey's motor-vehicle software registered about 6,600 people who had said they were noncitizens (June 2023 – June 2024); fewer than 400 voted
  • 266. The Justice Department's own published "findings" from the files it got
  • 267. States that refused the federal demand run the checks themselves
  • 268. Oct 9, 2026: a federal judge in Washington, D.C. vacated the Justice Department's whole "Voter List Maintenance Policy" nationwide
  • 269. The Supreme Court's Sept 25, 2026 SAVE stay and the Oct 9 vacatur are two different things
  • 270. The grounds in the dismissals, beyond the two families already logged
  • 271. Since Oct 1, 2026: the Oct 9 vacatur; Delaware, Hawaii, Idaho and Utah still pending; the deadline to seek Supreme Court review of the Michigan ruling is Nov 12
  • 272. North Carolina: the Justice Department's own HAVA suit led to a consent judgment to fill in missing ID numbers, with no one removed for it
  • 273. List-maintenance suits by private groups have won real changes
  • 274. Cross-state checking has thinned
  • 275. The law the refusing states cite is real, and so far every court agrees with them on the department's power
  • 276. Wrongly flagged citizens are a documented pattern
  • 277. Refusing the Justice Department is not refusing checks
  • Case file: "statistically impossible" — late-night ballot batches
  • 278. Pennsylvania and Wisconsin still cannot start processing mail ballots before Election Day
  • 279. Michigan changed its rules after 2020; Georgia lets counties start weeks early
  • 280. "Central count" cities report all their absentee ballots in one block, so the overnight jump is one update, not a trickle
  • 281. Mail voters and in-person voters split differently, so a late mail block leans one way — documented, not inferred
  • 282. The shift is not one-directional: where mail is pre-processed, early returns lean the other way
  • 283. Milwaukee, Nov 4, 2020, about 3:30 a.m.: the city's absentee results, about 170,000 ballots, in one update — and not all for one side
  • 284. Michigan, Wednesday morning 2020: four "spikes" cast "solely" for one candidate, per a sworn affidavit
  • 285. Milwaukee 2024, about 4 a.m.: "nearly 90%" of the absentee batch for one candidate
  • 286. Counting was much faster in 2024 where the rules changed; Wisconsin, which didn't change, was no faster
  • 287. Real overnight problems exist, and the record shows how they were caught
  • Case file: "statistically impossible" — more than 100% turnout
  • 288. A Michigan affidavit's "more than 100% turnout" precincts were in Minnesota
  • 289. The corrected Michigan list, filed weeks later, was still wrong
  • 290. One "more than 100%" figure was in an official county report, and it was a typo in the registered-voter count, not the votes
  • 291. Wisconsin's "more votes than registered voters" used an old registration figure
  • 292. "Seven Milwaukee wards had more votes than registered voters": the county's own ward results say no
  • 293. Pennsylvania's "205,000 more votes than voters" compared certified ballots with a registration database counties had not finished updating
  • 294. Real over-voting exists, at a scale of dozens, and gets caught
  • Case file: Detroit 2020 — "more votes than voters" in the absentee counting boards
  • 295. What "out of balance" means: the names in the poll book or absentee list do not match the ballots counted, and the gap is not explained
  • 296. Out-of-balance precincts cannot be recounted
  • 297. Detroit's imbalance ran the other way from "more votes than voters"
  • 298. After the county canvass, about 7 in 10 of Detroit's 134 absentee counting boards were out of balance
  • 299. How big the imbalances were at canvass: most boards off by four or fewer; a few hundred ballots in total
  • 300. Detroit was not unique
  • 301. The Wayne County board deadlocked 2–2, then certified 4–0 with an audit request; the stated reason for the "no" votes was the poll books
  • 302. The state board certified Michigan on Nov 23, 2020, 3–0 with one abstention
  • 303. After the state audit, 14 of 134 boards remained out of balance, each by five ballots or fewer
  • 304. The audit's fraud finding, in its own words
  • 305. Detroit's audit: "17 out of 174,000" and "83% balanced"
  • 306. The Republican-led Senate committee: the imbalances were "marginal", and fewer than in earlier elections
  • 307. 2016: worse, audited, human error
  • 308. The August 2020 primary: about the same share of absentee boards out (72%), which is why the state stepped in before November
  • 309. Detroit's counting boards balanced better (or worse) in 2022 and 2024
  • Case file: "statistically impossible" — one-sided batches and zero-vote precincts
  • 310. Shiawassee County, Michigan, Nov 4, 2020: "138,339 votes for one candidate, zero for the other" was a typo
  • 311. Philadelphia, Nov 4, 2020: "23,277 votes, all for one candidate" was one candidate's numbers entered before the other's
  • 312. "Votes switched" and "votes deleted" in the media data feed: rounding
  • 313. Philadelphia 2012: "59 divisions" with zero votes for the runner-up, a "mathematical and statistical impossibility"
  • 314. Philadelphia 2020: one division at zero
  • 315. It happens on the other side too: a Rockland County, New York precinct gave the 2024 winner 552 votes and the runner-up zero
  • 316. What one-sided precincts look like statistically, and when they would be a real red flag
  • Case file: what statistics can and can't prove
  • 317. The peer-reviewed review of 2020's statistical claims: each "anomaly" was either not a fact or not anomalous
  • 318. The "one in a quadrillion" claim tested whether two different elections were the same, and by that test nearly every election is "fraud"
  • 319. Benford's law on precinct vote counts says nothing about fraud
  • 320. What a real statistical red flag looked like: North Carolina's 9th District, 2018
  • Case file: Pennsylvania's undated mail ballots
  • 321. Pennsylvania law requires the mail voter to "fill out, date and sign" the declaration on the return envelope
  • 322. The handwritten date is not used to judge timeliness: ballots are stamped on receipt, and the deadline is receipt by 8 p.m. on Election Day
  • 323. The date catches fraud: the Lancaster County ballot dated after the voter had died
  • 324. A federal appeals court held the date immaterial under federal law in 2022; the U.S. Supreme Court then vacated that judgment
  • 325. The state Supreme Court's Ball v. Chapman ruling on undated ballots came in Nov 2022
  • 326. A 2024 federal appeals ruling held the federal civil-rights statute does not reach rules about casting a ballot, leaving the date rule standing
  • 327. 2024 in the state courts: the high court vacated and stayed lower-court rulings, then on Nov 18, 2024 ordered all counties not to count undated ballots
  • 328. The state Supreme Court has since ruled on the date rule under the state constitution
  • 329. The rule for Nov 3, 2026: counties may not reject a mail ballot over a missing or wrong date
  • 330. "The courts keep flip-flopping"
  • 331. 2022: more than 10,000 timely ballots were set aside statewide over the date
  • 332. 2024: about 4,500 ballots statewide
  • 333. Across the years: "tens of thousands"
  • 334. After the Nov 2024 election, the Bucks, Montgomery and Philadelphia boards voted to count undated and misdated ballots despite the state Supreme Court's rulings; Centre County did too
  • 335. The court's response: two concurring statements rebuked local officials
  • 336. Were those ballots counted in the end? No
  • 337. The comparator: the Senate race margin, about 15,000 votes
  • 338. A county commissioner said "precedent by a court doesn't matter anymore" about counting the undated ballots
  • 339. National framing on the "illegal ballots" side
  • 340. The "tossing legal votes" side
  • 341. How far the clip and the posts reached
  • 342. Undated ballots are fraud, or come from ineligible or late voters
  • Case file: noncitizen and other ineligible voting, and the roll-cleaning push
  • 343. The reading rule for every state number: registered is not voted, flagged is not confirmed
  • 344. Iowa: 2,176 flagged, 277 confirmed noncitizens, 35 counted ballots in the 2024 general
  • 345. Michigan, 2024: 15 noncitizen votes = 0.00028% of 5.7 million ballots
  • 346. Michigan's outcomes, and the state auditor's 2026 review
  • 347. Ohio, Aug 2024: 597 referred (138 voted, 459 registered only) of more than 8 million registered
  • 348. Ohio's earlier referrals: 521 noncitizen referrals, one noncitizen charged
  • 349. Ohio, Oct 2025: 1,084 noncitizens referred to the Justice Department, 167 "appear to have" voted in a federal election since 2018
  • 350. North Carolina, 2016: 41 noncitizens voted, all lawfully present, out of nearly 4.8 million voters
  • 351. Utah: 14 of the 27 confirmed noncitizens ticked "No" on the citizenship question and were registered anyway
  • 352. Philadelphia, 2006–2017: 220 noncitizens registered, 90 voted, 227 votes — most through motor-voter
  • 353. Pennsylvania statewide: up to 544 ballots in 93 million (2000–2017)
  • 354. Texas 2019: the "95,000" list, about 25,000 of them citizens, rescinded in a settlement
  • 355. Texas, 2021–2024: more than 6,500 "potential noncitizens" removed, about 1,930 with voting history
  • 356. Alabama 2024: 3,251 flagged; at least 2,074 of them eligible; a handful of noncitizens; the program was enjoined
  • 357. Virginia: 6,303 removed in 2022–24 without saying how many were noncitizens; about 1,600 removed Aug–Oct 2024, citizens among them
  • 358. The Supreme Court's Virginia order, Oct 30, 2024, was a stay, not a merits ruling
  • 359. Arizona 2024: about 218,000 long-time voters had no proof of citizenship on file, from a motor-vehicle coding error
  • 360. Oregon 2024–25: about 1,800 people registered by motor-vehicle error; 38 with any voting history, many of them citizens
  • 361. Florida 2012, for the pattern: 180,000 suspected, then 2,600, then 198
  • 362. New Hampshire, 1998–2024: 8 possible noncitizen voters in about 8.3 million votes
  • 363. Who the noncitizen voters are: overwhelmingly lawful residents, not people without legal status
  • 364. A 2014 survey estimate that 6.4% of noncitizens voted in 2008
  • 365. The rebuttal: it was measurement error
  • 366. Brennan Center, 2017: 42 jurisdictions, 23.5 million votes, about 30 suspected noncitizen votes referred
  • 367. Federal and database counts: about a hundred Heritage entries in 25 years; 129 federal prosecutions under the noncitizen-voting statute since 1996
  • 368. The federal crimes and penalties
  • 369. A noncitizen who votes unlawfully is deportable and inadmissible
  • 370. Where noncitizens may lawfully vote: some local elections only
  • 371. The March 2025 executive order requiring documentary proof of citizenship on the federal form, and the courts
  • 372. The SAVE America Act passed the House 218–213 on Feb 11, 2026, and stalled in the Senate
  • 373. The Supreme Court will hear Arizona's proof-of-citizenship case on Dec 8, 2026
  • 374. State proof-of-citizenship laws as of 2026
  • 375. Kansas, 2013–2016: 31,089 applicants denied under a documentary-proof law, against at most 39 noncitizens registered in the 14 years before
  • 376. Blocked citizens against caught noncitizens: the ratio depends on the method
  • 377. People with felony convictions voting where barred
  • 378. Double voting across states is rare and overcounted by name-and-birthdate matching
  • 379. "Dead people voted": a handful, usually relatives — and most "dead voter" counts are people who voted, then died

  • Case file: the Postal Service rule — "the post office won't deliver mail ballots to states that don't hand over voter rolls"
  • 380. The head of the Postal Service said the Postal Service would not mail ballots for states that refuse to hand over their voter rolls
  • 381. The instrument that started it: Executive Order 14399 (signed Mar 31, 2026)
  • 382. The rule: proposed June 2, 2026, final Aug 26, 2026; non-compliant outgoing ballot mailings "will not be accepted"
  • 383. The rule requires states' voter rolls
  • 384. The rule targets the states that refused the Justice Department

  • Case file: Arizona 2022 — printer failures, "35,563 injected ballots", signatures, Cochise County
  • 389. The certified governor's margin was 17,117 votes
  • 390. On Election Day, printers failed at about 70 of 223 vote centers, and 16,724 ballots went into "Door 3" to be counted centrally — all were counted
  • 391. An independent investigation by a retired state chief justice found the primary cause was equipment failure
  • 392. The losing candidate's election contest lost at trial, on appeal and in the state Supreme Court; a remand trial found signatures were checked
  • 393. The "35,563 injected ballots" number was a gap between two exhibits, and the state Supreme Court sanctioned the claim that it was undisputed as false
  • 394. The county recorder's defamation suit ended in a default and then a confidential settlement
  • 395. The 2022 attorney general race: a recount cut the margin from 511 to 280 votes after one county found uncounted ballots
  • 396. Cochise County: two supervisors refused to certify, one pleaded guilty in 2023 to a misdemeanour
  • Case file: the other side's "stolen" claims — Florida 2000, Ohio 2004, 2016, Georgia 2018
  • 397. Florida 2000: certified margin 537 votes; the media consortium's review found the answer depends on the counting standard, and named no winner
  • 398. Ohio 2004: the losing party's own study and the exit pollsters found no evidence the machines or the count flipped the state
  • 399. Two Cuyahoga County workers were convicted over the 2004 recount in 2005 or 2006
  • 400. 2016: no evidence that any vote was changed or any voting machine manipulated
  • 401. Georgia 2018 governor: the losing candidate said under oath that the winner "won under the rules that were in place"; a federal court found two administrative defects but ruled for the state
  • 402. Belief that an election was stolen tracks who lost, on both sides
  • Case file: real problems turned into fraud stories, 2024–2026
  • 403. Lancaster County, 2024: "2,600 fake ballots, all written by the same person" was about 2,500 suspect registration forms, from several paid canvassers, caught before anyone voted
  • 404. California's June 2026 primary: a slow count and a late shift were the law working, not cheating
  • 405. Maryland's 2026 primary: "500,000 fake ballots" was a vendor sending some voters the wrong party's ballot; every mail voter was reissued one
  • 406. Venice, California, 2026: "26 registered voters at a vacant lot" was a former homeless shelter, which state law allows as a registration address
  • Case file: Wisconsin 2020, start to finish
  • 407. The night: one side led by more than 100,000 for much of the night; the lead flipped after 3:30 a.m. when Milwaukee reported its absentee ballots, then Green Bay and Kenosha
  • 408. What moved the state was also the suburbs

  • 415. The commission's guidance not to send special voting deputies into care facilities broke the statute; one prosecutor documented one facility

  • Case file: "so many articles can't all be fake"

  • 422. What courts found when they weighed sworn affidavits

  • 424. Many of the officials who checked had every political reason to find the opposite

  • 426. Who certified 2020 in the decisive states
  • 427. Poll workers are drawn from both major parties' lists and the public may observe (Wisconsin statute)

  • Case file: 2020 against 2024 — "where did 80 million come from, and where did the votes go?"
  • 435. The FEC's final 2020 totals: 81,283,501 and 74,223,975, of 158,429,631

  • Case file: the voting-machine company formerly called Dominion, 2025–2026

  • 471. The President pledged (August 2025) an executive order to get rid of voting machines
